# how to check git history of a file

You can check the Git history of a file using the `git log` command.

### The Core Commands

- **View basic history**: Use `git log` followed by your file path.\
  bash

<!---->

```
git log path/to/file.txt
```

Use code with caution.

- **Include renames**: Use `--follow` so Git tracks the file's history even if it was renamed or moved in the past.\
  bash

<!---->

```
git log --follow path/to/file.txt
```

Use code with caution.

- **See actual code changes (diffs)**: Add the `-p` or `--patch` flag to display the exact lines added or removed in each commit.\
  bash

<!---->

```
git log --follow -p path/to/file.txt
```

Use code with caution.\
[link](https://sentry.io/answers/view-the-change-history-of-a-single-file-in-git/)

***

### Advanced Filtering Options

| Goal                     | Command                                       | What it does                                                                     |
| ------------------------ | --------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Clean Overview**       | `git log --oneline path/to/file.txt`          | Condenses each commit to a single line containing its short SHA and message.     |
| **Line History**         | `git log -L 10,25:path/to/file.txt`           | Shows the history and evolution of specific lines (lines 10 to 25).              |
| **Function History**     | `git log -L :myFunctionName:path/to/file.txt` | Automatically traces the changes made strictly to a specific function or method. |
| **Line-by-Line Authors** | `git blame path/to/file.txt`                  | Shows exactly who modified each specific line of code last and in which commit.  |

***

### Alternative GUI & Web Methods

- **GitHub / GitLab**: Navigate to the file on the website and click the **History** button in the top right corner to browse changes in a visual interface.
- **VS Code**: Open the file, go to the **Timeline** panel at the bottom of the Explorer sidebar to view its Git history timeline seamlessly.
- **Built-in Git GUI**: Run `gitk path/to/file.txt` in your terminal to fire up Git's default visual history browser. [link](https://stackoverflow.com/questions/278192/view-the-change-history-of-a-file-using-git-versioning)

If you are looking for a **specific change** or trying to **revert a file**, let me know. I can give you the exact command to find a deleted text string or restore the file to a previous version. [link](https://coreui.io/answers/how-to-search-file-content-in-git-history/)

# (?) how to see all non text file changes in a git repo sorted by size

To see all non-text (binary) file changes in a Git repository sorted by size, you can combine Git commands with standard terminal sorting utilities.

Run the following command in your terminal:

bash

```
git diff --numstat | grep '^-' | awk '{print $3, "Size info not directly in numstat"}' # Run the optimized pipeline below:
```

Use code with caution.

Because Git's internal diff engine does not calculate exact byte sizes for binary changes directly in standard logs, the most reliable way to list and sort them by current disk size is using this command pipeline:

bash

```
git diff --name-only | git check-attr --stdin diff | grep 'diff: unset' | cut -d: -f1 | xargs -I {} du -b "{}" 2>/dev/null | sort -n
```

Use code with caution.

### Command Breakdown

- **`git diff --name-only`**: Lists all modified files.
- **`git check-attr --stdin diff`**: Checks Git attributes to identify binary files.
- **`grep 'diff: unset'`**: Filters only the files Git treats as binary.
- **`cut -d: -f1`**: Extracts the clean file paths.
- **`xargs -I {} du -b "{}"`**: Gets the exact size in bytes for each file.
- **`sort -n`**: Sorts the final list numerically from smallest to largest.

### Alternative: View Already Committed Binary Files

If you want to find the largest binary files across your **entire commit history** instead of just unstaged changes, use this command:

bash

```
git rev-list --objects --all | git cat-file --batch-check='%(objectsize) %(objectname) %(objectpath)' | grep -v ' $' | sort -n
```

Use code with caution.

To advance your goal, would you like to:

- Filter the list by a **specific file extension** (like `.png` or `.zip`)?

- View the sizes in a **human-readable format** (KB, MB)?

- Learn how to permanently **remove large binaries** from your Git history?

## no effect

If the previous commands showed no output, it means Git does not currently see those files as **modified binary files**, or the command broke on your specific Operating System (like macOS, which handles the `du` command differently).

Here is how to fix this based on what you are trying to achieve:

### Fix 1: If you are on macOS (Mac)

The `du -b` flag does not work on Mac. Use this adjusted command instead:

bash

```
git diff --name-only | git check-attr --stdin diff | grep 'diff: unset' | cut -d: -f1 | xargs -I {} du -k "{}" 2>/dev/null | sort -n
```

Use code with caution.

_Note: This sorts by kilobytes (KB) instead of bytes._

### Fix 2: If the files are already committed

If you already saved the changes into a commit, `git diff` will return nothing. To see non-text changes in your **last commit**, use this:

bash

```
git diff --name-only HEAD~1 HEAD | git check-attr --stdin diff | grep 'diff: unset' | cut -d: -f1 | xargs -I {} ls -lh "{}"
```

Use code with caution.

### Fix 3: If you just want to find all large files in the folder

If you want to find all non-text files sorted by size regardless of Git status, run this:

bash

```
find . -type f -not -path '*/.*' -exec file {} + | grep -v 'text' | cut -d: -f1 | xargs du -sh | sort -h
```

Use code with caution.

To narrow this down, let me know:

- Are you using **Mac, Windows, or Linux**?

- Are these files **already committed**, or just sitting in your folder?

- What **file types** (like `.png`, `.exe`, `.mp4`) are you expecting to see?

# how to see all currently non existing binary files in git history sorted by size on ubuntu

To list all deleted (currently non-existing) binary files in your Git history on Ubuntu and sort them by size, you can combine specific Git commands with standard Linux utilities like `awk` and `sort`.

Open your terminal and run the following command inside your Git repository:

bash

```
git log --all --full-history --numstat --format="%H" | \
awk '
NF==1 { commit=$1 }
NF==3 && $1=="-" && $2=="-" {
    file=$3;
    cmd="git show " commit ":" file " 2>/dev/null | wc -c";
    cmd | getline size;
    close(cmd);
    if (size != "") print size, file;
}' | \
while read -r size file; do
    [ ! -f "$file" ] && echo "$size $file";
done | sort -n -r | uniq
```

Use code with caution.

### How It Works

- **`git log --all --full-history --numstat`**: Scans all branches and history. The `--numstat` flag outputs `- -` for binary files instead of the number of lines added/deleted.
- **`awk ... wc -c`**: Identifies the binary files, matches them to their respective commit hashes, and extracts the exact file size in bytes directly from the Git object database.
- **`[ ! -f "$file" ]`**: Filters the list to only include files that do not currently exist in your working directory.
- **`sort -n -r | uniq`**: Sorts the results numerically (`-n`) in reverse order (`-r`) so the largest files appear first, and removes duplicate entries.

To help you manage your repository size, let me know if you want to:

- **Completely purge** these large binary files from your entire Git history using `git filter-repo` to shrink your `.git` folder.

- **Convert** the tracking of large files to **Git LFS (Large File Storage)**.

- **Filter** the results to look for specific file extensions (like `.zip`, `.exe`, or `.png`).

## what does the number mean

The number output by that command represents the **exact file size in bytes** at the specific moment the file existed in your history.

For example, a number like `10485760` means the deleted binary file was exactly 10 Megabytes (MB).

If you want to make the output much easier to read, you can modify the final sorting step to convert those raw bytes into a human-readable format (like K, M, or G).

Run this updated version instead:

bash

```
git log --all --full-history --numstat --format="%H" | \
awk '
NF==1 { commit=$1 }
NF==3 && $1=="-" && $2=="-" {
    file=$3;
    cmd="git show " commit ":" file " 2>/dev/null | wc -c";
    cmd | getline size;
    close(cmd);
    if (size != "") print size, file;
}' | \
while read -r size file; do
    [ ! -f "$file" ] && echo "$size $file";
done | sort -n -r | uniq | numfmt --to=iec
```

Use code with caution.

### What Changed?

- **`numfmt --to=iec`**: This Ubuntu utility automatically converts the raw byte number (e.g., `15428900`) into an easily scannable format (e.g., `15M`).

If you're tracking down these files, would you like to:

- See **which specific commit** originally deleted each file?
- Know how to **restore** one of these deleted binary files back into your project?
- Learn how to check if these files are the reason your **`.git` folder size** is so large?

## why are some numbers zero

The number is zero <mark>because the command found a<span> </span><strong>completely empty binary file</strong><span> </span>(0 bytes) or encountered an<span> </span><strong>untrackable file state</strong></mark> (like a deleted git submodule or a broken symlink).

Here are the three reasons why a size can return as zero:

### 1. The File Was Empty

A developer may have created a blank placeholder file (e.g., `touch empty_image.png`) and committed it to the repository before it was later deleted. Git tracks these as valid objects, but their size is `0` bytes.

### 2. Git Submodules or Symlinks

If a folder was added as a Git submodule or a symbolic link and then deleted, `--numstat` sometimes registers it as a binary change (`- -`). However, because it is a link pointing to an external repository or path rather than a physical file inside your database, `git show` outputs nothing, resulting in `0` bytes.

### 3. File Renames or Mode Changes

If a binary file was renamed, moved, or had its file permissions changed (e.g., made executable) right before it was deleted, the `git log` history might flag the old path as an empty binary artifact during that specific commit transition.

### How to filter out the zeros

If the zero-byte entries are cluttering your results, you can automatically hide them by adding a quick size check (`if (size > 0)`) to the command.

Run this version to **only show files larger than zero bytes**:

bash

```
git log --all --full-history --numstat --format="%H" | \
awk '
NF==1 { commit=$1 }
NF==3 && $1=="-" && $2=="-" {
    file=$3;
    cmd="git show " commit ":" file " 2>/dev/null | wc -c";
    cmd | getline size;
    close(cmd);
    if (size != "" && size > 0) print size, file;
}' | \
while read -r size file; do
    [ ! -f "$file" ] && echo "$size $file";
done | sort -n -r | uniq | numfmt --to=iec
```

Use code with caution.

If you are trying to clean up your repository, let me know if you want to:

- Investigate a **specific zero-byte file** to see what type of object it actually was.
- Find out **who committed** these files into the history.
- Set up a **`.gitignore` file** to prevent empty or accidental binary files from being tracked in the future.

## make diff rename limit infinite

To make the Git diff rename limit infinite, you need to set the configuration value to `0`. In Git, a rename limit value of `0` tells the system to use no limit at all.

You can apply this change in two different ways depending on your needs.

### Option 1: Apply Globally (Recommended)

This changes the setting for **all repositories** on your Ubuntu system. Run this command in your terminal:

bash

```
git config --global diff.renameLimit 0
```

Use code with caution.

### Option 2: Apply to the Current Repository Only

This changes the setting **only for the project folder** you are currently inside. Run this command inside your repository:

bash

```
git config diff.renameLimit 0
```

Use code with caution.

### Why Do This?

By default, Git stops looking for renamed files if a commit modifies too many files (usually more than 400 or 1000 files). Setting this to `0` forces Git to thoroughly scan every single file change to see if it was renamed, which prevents your binary file history scripts from accidentally misidentifying moved files as "deleted" or empty.

_Note: For very massive repositories with tens of thousands of changed files in a single commit, setting this to infinite can cause Git commands to run noticeably slower while it calculates the matches._

Now that the rename limit is infinite, would you like to:

- Re-run the **binary file size script** to see if any zero-byte files or duplicates disappeared?

- Check your **current Git configurations** to confirm the setting applied properly?

- Increase the **merge rename limit** (`merge.renameLimit`) as well to handle complex code branches?

 ~/Documents/f % git log --all --full-history --numstat --format="%H" | \
awk '
NF==1 { commit=$1 }
NF==3 && $1=="-" && $2=="-" {
    file=$3;
    cmd="git show " commit ":" file " 2>/dev/null | wc -c";
    cmd | getline size;
    close(cmd);
    if (size != "") print size, file;
}' | \
while read -r size file; do
    [ ! -f "$file" ] && echo "$size $file";
done | sort -n -r | uniq | numfmt --to=iec

 ~/Documents/f % git log --all --full-history --numstat --format="%H" | \
awk '
NF==1 { commit=$1 }
NF==3 && $1=="-" && $2=="-" {
    file=$3;
    cmd="git show " commit ":" file " 2>/dev/null | wc -c";
    cmd | getline size;
    close(cmd);
    if (size != "") print size, file;
}' | \
while read -r size file; do
    [ ! -f "$file" ] && echo "$size $file";
done | sort -n -r | uniq | numfmt --to=iec

 ~/Documents/f % git log --all --full-history --numstat --format="%H" | \
awk '
NF==1 { commit=$1 }
NF==3 && $1=="-" && $2=="-" {
    file=$3;
    cmd="git show " commit ":" file " 2>/dev/null | wc -c";
    cmd | getline size;
    close(cmd);
    if (size != "" && size > 0) print size, file;
}' | \
while read -r size file; do
    [ ! -f "$file" ] && echo "$size $file";
done | sort -n -r | uniq | numfmt --to=iec
